The Algebras for Automatic Relations Thanks: Licensed under CC BY 4.0 .
Rémi Morvan

Abstract
We introduce “synchronous algebras”, an algebraic structure tailored to recognize automatic relations ( a.k.a. synchronous relations, or regular relations). They are the equivalent of monoids for regular languages, however they conceptually differ in two points: first, they are typed and second, they are equipped with a dependency relation expressing constraints between elements of different types. The interest of the proposed definition is that it allows to lift, in an effective way, pseudovarieties of regular languages to that of synchronous relations, and we show how algebraic characterizations of pseudovarieties of regular languages can be lifted to the pseudovarieties of synchronous relations that they induce. Since this construction is effective, this implies that the membership problem is decidable for (infinitely) many natural classes of automatic relations. A typical example of such a pseudovariety is the class of “group relations”, defined as the relations recognized by finite-state synchronous permutation automata. In order to prove this result, we adapt two pillars of algebraic language theory to synchronous algebras: (a) any relation admits a syntactic synchronous algebra recognizing it, and moreover, the relation is synchronous if, and only if, its syntactic algebra is finite and (b) classes of synchronous relations with desirable closure properties ( i.e. pseudovarieties) correspond to pseudovarieties of synchronous algebras.

1
Introduction

1.1
Background

The landscape of rationality for
k
-ary relations of finite words (
k\geq 2
) is far more complex than for languages—recall that languages can be seen as unary relations of finite words—as depicted in
Figure

3
on page
3
. Perhaps the most natural class is that of
rational relations
, defined as relations accepted by non-deterministic two-tape automata—an input
(u,v)
is described by writing
u
on the first tape and
v
and the second tape—that can move its two heads independently, from left to right—see
[
CCG06
, 2.1]
for a formal definition. For instance, the suffix relation is
rational
.

Our paper focuses on
synchronous relations
,
a.k.a.

automatic relations
or
regular relations
, defined as the
rational relations
that can be recognized by

synchronous automata
,
a subclass of the machines described above obtained by keeping a single head that
moves synchronously from left to right, reading one pair of letters after the other; we add
padding symbols

at the end of the shorter word—see

Figure

1
.
While the suffix relation is not
synchronous
, typical examples include the prefix relation,
the same-length relation, etc.

Synchronous relations
play a central role in the
definitions of automatic structures—introduced by Hodgson
[
Hod76
,
Hod82
,
Hod83
]
and rediscovered by Khoussainov & Nerode
[
KN95
]
, see
[
Blu23
, XI, pp. 627–762]
.
They also have been studied in the context of graph databases
[
BLLW12
, Definition 3.1, p.7 & Theorem 6.3, p. 13]
, see
[
Fig21
, 8, p. 17]
for more context & results on
extended
conjunctive regular path queries.

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right),\textrm{Pad}\hskip 12.2917pt
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right)
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right)
\hskip 12.2917pt\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right),\textrm{Pad}

Encoding a pair of words of
\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}
into an element of

({\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!)^{*}
where
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!\mathrel{\hat{=}}(\Sigma\times\Sigma)\,\cup\,(\Sigma\times\{\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\})\,\cup\,(\{\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\times\Sigma)
(left) and
a deterministic complete
synchronous automaton
(right)
over
\Sigma=\{a,b\}
accepting the binary relation
of pairs
(u,v)
such that the number of
a
’s in
u_{1}\ldots u_{k}

and in
v_{1}\ldots v_{k}
are the same mod
2
, where
k=\min(|u|,|v|)
.

Pad
denotes the set of transitions
\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right)\}
.

Remark 1
.

All our results are described for binary relations, but can be extended to

k
-ary synchronous relations, see
Section

5
.

Synchronous relations
stand at the frontier between expressiveness and undecidability: for instance, Carton, Choffrut and Grigorieff showed that it is decidable whether an
automatic relation
is
recognizable

[
CCG06
, Proposition 3.9, p. 265]
, meaning that
it can be written as a finite union of Cartesian products of regular languages.
1
1

1

For
instance, the relation “having the same length modulo
2
” is
recognizable
, since it can be
written as
(aa)^{*}\times(aa)^{*}\cup a(aa)^{*}\times a(aa)^{*}
.
2
2

2

The problem was latter shown to be
NL
-complete and

PSpace
-complete depending on whether the input automaton is deterministic or not
in
[
BHL+19
, Theorem 1, p. 3]
.

Synchronous relations
are effectively closed under Boolean operations—see
e.g.

[
Blu23
, Lemma XI.1.3, p. 627]
,
and moreover, inclusion (and subsequent problems: universality, emptiness, equivalence…) is decidable for them,
by reduction to classical automata,
contrary to the equivalence problem over
rational relations

which is undecidable
[
Ber79
, Theorem 8.4, p. 81]
.

However, some seemingly easy problems are undecidable: Köcher showed that
it is undecidable if the (infinite) graph defined by a
synchronous relation
is
2-colourable—
[
Kö14
, Proposition 6.5, p. 43]
, and Barceló, Figueira and Morvan showed that undecidability also
holds for regular 2-colourability
[
BFM23
, Theorem 4.4, p. 8]
.
On the other hand, one can decide
if said graph contains an infinite clique, see
[
KL10
, Corollary 5.5, p. 32]
:
this is a consequence of
[
Rub08
, Theorem 3.20, p. 185]
.

1.2
Motivation

Any
synchronous relation
can be seen as a regular language over the alphabet

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!\mathrel{\hat{=}}(\Sigma\times\Sigma)\,\cup\,(\Sigma\times\{\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\})\,\cup\,(\{\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\times\Sigma)
of pairs. On the other hand any regular language
L
over
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!

produces a
synchronous relation
when intersected with the language of all

well-formed words
—namely words where the padding symbols are consistently placed;
see
Section

2
for precise definitions. In fact, the semantics
of
synchronous automata
such as the one in
Figure

1
is precisely defined this way:
it is the intersection of the “classical semantic” of the automaton, seen as an NFA, intersected
with
well-formed words
.

Figure 2:

Drawing in
({\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!)^{*}
of a
\mathcal{V}
-relation

\mathcal{R}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\neg}\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\hat{=}}\{(u,v)\in\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}\mid(u,v)\not\in\mathcal{R}\}
, where
\mathcal{R}
is defined as
L\cap{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\textsf{WellFormed}_{\Sigma}}
with
L\in\mathcal{V}
.

In particular, a class
\mathcal{V}
of regular languages over

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!

(
e.g.
first-order definable languages, group languages, etc.) induces a class of so-called

\mathcal{V}
-relations
, defined as the relations over
\Sigma
obtained as the intersection of some
language of
\mathcal{V}
with
well-formed words
, see
Figure

2
.
For instance, the relation of
Figure

1

is a
\mathcal{V}
-relation
where
\mathcal{V}
is the class of all group languages—these relations can be
alternatively described as those recognized by a deterministic complete synchronous automaton whose
transitions functions are permutations of states.

Question 2
.

Given a class
\mathcal{V}
of languages, can we characterize and decide the class of
\mathcal{V}
-relations
?

As we will see in
Example

6
, for a
relation
to be
\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.22083pt\raisebox{1.22496pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!}

is not necessary for it to be a
\mathcal{V}
-relation
.

1.3
Contributions

We answer positively to this question.
For this we first need to develop an algebraic theory of
synchronous relations
,
which enables us to prove the lifting theorem. In short, the
lifting theorem
states that algebraic characterizations of classes of word languages can be lifted in a canonical way to algebraic characterizations of classes of word relations.

The algebraic approach usually provides more than decidability: it attaches
canonical algebras to languages/relations (
e.g.
monoids for languages of finite words), and often simple ways to characterize complex properties (
e.g.
first-order definability, see
e.g.

[
Boj20
, Theorem 2.6, p. 40]
).
Our
synchronous algebras
differ from monoids in two points:

•

they are typed—a quite common feature in algebraic language theory, shared
e.g.
by
\omega
-semigroups
[
PP04
, 4.1, p. 91]
;

•

they are equipped with a
dependency relation
, which expresses constraints between
elements of different types---to our knowledge, this feature is entirely novel.
3
3

3

Note that algebras equipped with binary relations have been studied before,
e.g.
Pin’s ordered

\omega
-semigroups—see
[
Pin98
, 2.4, p. 7]
—but the constraints (here the
orderings) are always defined between elements of the
same type
.

Importantly, some variations are possible on the definition of
synchronous algebras
:
in particular, one could get rid of the notion of
dependency relation
and

Lemmas

18
and
27
would still hold.
However, we show in
Appendix

F
that these
simplified synchronous algebras cannot characterize the property of being a
\mathcal{V}
-relation
.
Therefore, the notion of
dependency
seems necessary to tackle
Question

2
.
Moreover, we show that these algebras arise from a monad, but to our knowledge none of the
meta-theorems developing algebraic language theories over monads apply to it,
see
Appendix

A
for more details.

We show that assuming that
\mathcal{V}
is a
*
-pseudovariety of regular languages
—in short, a class of regular languages with desirable closure properties—, then the algebraic characterization of
\mathcal{V}
can be easily lifted to characterize
\mathcal{V}
-relations
.

{restatable*}

[
Lifting theorem: Elementary Formulation
]theoremliftingtheoremmonoids

Given a
relation

\mathcal{R}
and a
\ast
-pseudovariety of regular languages

\mathcal{V}

corresponding
to a
pseudovariety of monoids

\mathbb{V}
,
the following are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{R}
is a
\mathcal{V}
-relation
,

2.

\mathcal{R}
is
recognized
by a finite
synchronous algebra

\mathbf{A}

whose
underlying monoids
are all in
\mathbb{V}
,

3.

all
underlying monoids
of the
syntactic synchronous algebras

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{A}_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}}
of

\mathcal{R}
are in
\mathbb{V}
.

This theorem rests on a solid algebraic theory.
First, we show the existence of
syntactic algebras
(
Lemma

18
):
each relation
\mathcal{R}
admits a unique canonical and minimal algebra
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{A}_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}}
, which is finite

iff
the relation is
synchronous
,
and then, we exhibit a correspondence between classes of finite algebras and classes of
synchronous relations (
Lemma

27
)—we assume suitable closure properties; these classes are called “pseudovarieties”.
While the proof structures of
Lemmas

18
and
27
follow the classic proofs, see
e.g.

[
Pin22
]
,
the
dependency relation
has to be taken into account quite carefully, leading for instance
to a surprising definition of
residuals
, see
Definition

25
.

Organization.

After giving preliminary results in
Section

2
, we introduce
the
synchronous algebras
in
Section

3
and show the existence of

syntactic algebras
. We then proceed to prove the
lifting theorem
for

*
-pseudovarieties
in
Section

4
, and after introducing
*
-pseudovarieties of synchronous relations
, we provide a more algebraic reformulation of the
lifting
theorem
(
Theorem

29
).
We conclude the paper with
a short discussion in
Section

5
.

1.4
Related Work

Figure 3:

The landscape of rationality for binary relations.
Dashed regions are empty: the intersection of
functional relations and two-way rational relations
collapses to regular functions by

[
EH01
, Theorem 22, p. 243]
.

The algebraic framework has been extended far beyond languages of finite words: let us cite amongst other Reutenauer’s “algèbre associative syntactique” for weighted languages

[
Reu80
, Théorème I.2.1, p. 451]
and their associated Eilenberg theorem
[
Reu80
, Théorème III.1.1, p. 469]
;
for languages of
\omega
-words, Wilke’s algebras and
\omega
-semigroups,
see
[
PP04
, II, pp. 75–131 & VI, pp. 265–306]
;
more generally, for languages over countable linear orderings, see Carton, Colcombet & Puppis’ “
\circledast
-monoids” and “
\circledast
-algebras”

[
CCP18
, 3, p. 7]
.
A systemic approach has been recently developed using monads, see
Appendix

A
.
Non-linear structures are also suited to such an approach, see
e.g.
Bojańczyk & Walukiewicz’s
forest algebras
[
BW08
, 1.3, p. 4]

[
Boj20
, 5, p. 159]
, or Engelfriet’s hyperedge replacement algebras for graph languages

[
CE12
, 2.3, p. 100]

[
Boj15
, 6.2, p. 194]
.
For relations over words (
a.k.a.
transductions),
recognizable
relations
are exactly the ones recognized by monoid morphisms
\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}\to M

where
M
is finite. This can be trivially generalized to show
that a relation
\mathcal{R}
is a finite union of Cartesian products of languages in
\mathcal{V}
if, and only
if, it is recognized by a monoid from
\mathbb{V}
, the pseudovariety of monoids corresponding to

\mathcal{V}
, see
Appendix

B
.
In 2023, Bojańczyk & Nguyễn
managed to develop an algebraic structure called “transducer semigroups” for “regular functions”
[
BNê23
, Theorem 3.2, p. 6]
, an
orthogonal class of relations to ours—see
Figure

3
.

The counterpart of
\mathcal{V}
-relations
for rational relations—that we call here
\mathcal{V}
-rational relations—was studied by Filiot, Gauwin & Lhote
[
FGL19
]
: they show that if

\mathcal{V}
has decidable membership, then “
\mathcal{V}
-rational transductions” also have decidable membership

[
FGL19
, Theorem 4.10, p. 26]
.
“Rational transductions” correspond in
Figure

3
to the intersection of functional relations with rational relations: this class
is orthogonal to
synchronous relations
,
but is included in the class of “regular functions”.
A different problem—focussing more on the semantics of the transduction—, called “
\mathcal{V}
-continuity” was studied by Cadilhac, Carton & Paperman
[
CCP20
, Theorem 1.3, p. 3]
, although it has to be noted that their results only concern
a finite number of pseudovarieties.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Automata & Relations

We assume familiarity with basic algebraic language theory over finite words, see
[
Boj20
, 1, 2, 4, pp. 3–66 & pp. 107–156]
for a succinct and monad-driven approach, or
[
Pin22
, I–XIV, pp. 3–247]
for a more detailed presentation of the domain.
We also refer to
[
SW21
]
for a presentation on pseudovarieties.
4
4

4

“Pseudovarieties of
foo
” and “varieties of finite
foo
”—where
foo
is
e.g.
“groups”
or “semigroups”—are used interchangeably in the literature.

More precise pointers are given in
Appendix

C
.

A
relation
is a subset of
\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}
,
where
\Sigma
is an alphabet—
i.e.
a non-empty finite set.
We define its
complement

\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\neg}\mathcal{R}
as the
relation

\{(u,v)\in\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}\mid(u,v)\not\in\mathcal{R}\}
.
Letting

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!\mathrel{\hat{=}}(\Sigma\times\Sigma)\,\cup\,(\Sigma\times\{\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\})\,\cup\,(\{\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\times\Sigma)
, a
synchronous automaton
is a finite-state machine with initial states, final states, and
non-deterministic transitions labelled by elements of
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!
.
We denote by
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\textsf{WellFormed}_{\Sigma}}
the set of
well-formed
words over
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!
where the padding symbols are placed consistently, namely: if some padding symbol occurs on a tape/component, then the following symbols of this tape/component must all be padding symbols.
From this constraint, and since
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)\not\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!
,
there can never be padding symbols on both tapes.

Note that elements of
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\textsf{WellFormed}_{\Sigma}}
are in natural bijection with
\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}
—see

Figure

1
.
The relation recognized by a
synchronous automaton
is the set of pairs
(u,v)\in\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}
such that their corresponding element in
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\textsf{WellFormed}_{\Sigma}}
is the label of
an accepting run of the automaton. We say that a relation is
synchronous
if it is recognized
by such a machine.

Remark 3
.

Crucially, in the semantics of
synchronous automata
we
never

try to feed them inputs where the padding symbols are not consistent: for instance, while

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}aab\\
b\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pta\end{smallmatrix}\right),\text{ or }\left(\begin{smallmatrix}aba\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\\
a\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974ptb\end{smallmatrix}\right)

are sequences in
({\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.51994pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.42224pt\rule{2.53339pt}{0.6333pt}\hskip 0.42224pt
}}}}\!)^{*}
, the behaviour of a
synchronous automaton

on such sequences is completely disregarded to define the relation it recognizes.

We can then reformulate the definition of the semantics of a
synchronous automaton
,
to make the connection with
\mathcal{V}
-relations
—see the next subsection—explicit.

Fact 4
.

Given a
synchronous automaton
, its semantics as a
synchronous automaton

can be written as the intersection of its semantics as a classical automaton over
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.51994pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.42224pt\rule{2.53339pt}{0.6333pt}\hskip 0.42224pt
}}}}\!

with
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\textsf{WellFormed}_{\Sigma}}
.

In particular a relation
\mathcal{R}
is
synchronous
if, and only if, it is a regular language when seen as a subset of
({\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!)^{*}
.

2.2
Induced Relations

Given a class
\mathcal{V}
of regular languages,
the class of
\mathcal{V}
-relations
over
\Sigma
consists of all
relations

of the form
L\cap{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\textsf{WellFormed}_{\Sigma}}
for some
L\in\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.22083pt\raisebox{1.22496pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!}
—see
Figure

2
.

5

L\in\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.22083pt\raisebox{1.22496pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt

the alphabet
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!
. See
[

, introduction of XIII.1]

for why classes of regular languages are defined in such a way.

Figure 4:

Minimal (deterministic complete) “classical” automaton for
the binary relation
of pairs
(u,v)
such that the number of
a
’s in
u_{1}\ldots u_{k}

and in
v_{1}\ldots v_{k}
are the same mod
2
, where
k=\min(|u|,|v|)
,
seen as a language over
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!
.
Said otherwise, this is automaton rejects exactly all words in
({\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!)^{*}
which (1)
are not the valid encoding of a pair of words and (2) are the encoding of a pair
which does not satisfy the property above.
Each label
*
is defined so that the automaton is deterministic and complete.

For instance, if
\mathcal{V}
is the class of all regular languages, then by

Fact

4
,
\mathcal{V}
-relations
are exactly the
regular relations
,
a.k.a.

synchronous relations
!
However, because of

Remark

3
, the minimal automaton for a relation, seen as a language over
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!
,
can be significantly more complex than a deterministic complete
synchronous automaton
recognizing it, see
Figure

4
in page
4
—while the size blow-up is only polynomial, it breaks many of the structural properties of the automaton, such as the property of being a permutation automaton.

Note that if
\mathcal{R}
belongs to
\mathcal{V}
when
\mathcal{R}
is seen as a language over
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!
,
then
\mathcal{R}
is a
\mathcal{V}
-relation
.
The converse implication holds under some strong assumption on
\mathcal{V}
(
Fact

5
),
but is not true in general (
Example

6
).

Fact 5
.

If
\mathcal{V}
is a class of languages closed under intersection and that contains
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\textsf{WellFormed}_{\Sigma}}
, then
a relation
\mathcal{R}
is a
\mathcal{V}
-relation
if, and only if, it belongs to
\mathcal{V}
when seen
as a language over
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.51994pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.42224pt\rule{2.53339pt}{0.6333pt}\hskip 0.42224pt
}}}}\!
.

Classes of languages
\mathcal{V}
satisfying the previous assumption (
e.g.
first-order definable languages, piecewise-testable languages, etc.) are easy to capture when
it comes to
\mathcal{V}
-relations
since this class reduces to
\mathcal{V}
-languages.
So, in the remaining of the paper, we will focus on classes
\mathcal{V}
which do not satisfy
the assumptions of
Fact

5
, such as group languages.

Example 6

(Group relations)
.

If
\mathcal{V}
is the class
of group languages, namely languages recognized by permutation automata
6
6

6

A permutation
automaton is a finite-state deterministic complete automaton whose transition functions are all
permutations of states.
or equivalently by a finite group, then we call

\mathcal{V}
-relations
“
group relations
”. They can be characterized
as relations recognized by permutation
synchronous automata
. For instance, the relation
of
Figure

1
is a
group relation
as witnessed by the permutation synchronous automaton of
Figure

1
. Note however that it is not a group language, when seen as a language over
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.51994pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.42224pt\rule{2.53339pt}{0.6333pt}\hskip 0.42224pt
}}}}\!
, since its minimal automaton over
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.51994pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.42224pt\rule{2.53339pt}{0.6333pt}\hskip 0.42224pt
}}}}\!
is not
a permutation automaton, see
Figure

4
on
Figure

4
.

Fact 7
.

Given a relation
\mathcal{R}
and a class
\mathcal{V}
of languages, the following are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{R}
is a
\mathcal{V}
-relation
;

2.

\mathcal{R}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\neg}\mathcal{R}
are
\mathcal{V}
-separable as languages over
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.51994pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.42224pt\rule{2.53339pt}{0.6333pt}\hskip 0.42224pt
}}}}\!
,
i.e. there is a language in
\mathcal{V}
which contains
\mathcal{R}
and does not intersect
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\neg}\mathcal{R}
.

Proof.

By definition, see
Figure

2
, on page
2
.
∎

And so, if the
\mathcal{V}
-separability problem is decidable, then the class of
\mathcal{V}
-relations

is decidable. However, there are pseudovarieties
\mathcal{V}
with decidable membership but
undecidable separability problem
[
RS11
, Corollary 1.6, p. 478]
.
7
7

7

The paper cited only claims undecidability of pointlikes, but it was noted in
[
GS19
, 1, pp. 1–2]
that undecidability of the 2-pointlikes also holds, which is a problem
equivalent to separability by
[
Alm99
, Proposition 3.4, p. 6]
.

Moreover, some of these classes do not contain
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\textsf{WellFormed}_{\Sigma}}

[
RS11
, Corollary 1.7, p. 478]
. But beyond
this, even when a separation algorithm exists, it can be conceptually much harder than its
membership counterpart: for instance, deciding membership for group languages is trivial—it boils down to checking if a monoid is a group—, yet the decidability of the
separation problem for group languages is considered to be one of the major results in semigroup theory:
it follows from Ash’s infamous type II theorem
[
Ash91
, Theorem 2.1, p. 129]
, see
[
HMPR91
, Theorem 1.1, p. 3]
for a presentation of the result in terms of pointlike sets, see also
[
PZ23
, III, Theorem 8, p. 5]
for an elegant automata-theoretic reformulation.

3
Synchronous Algebras

In this section, we introduce and study the “elementary” properties of
synchronous algebras
.

3.1
Types & dependent Sets

Motivation.

The axiomatization of a semigroup reflects the algebraic structure of
finite words: these objects can be concatenated, in an associative way—reflecting the linearity of
words. Now observe that elements of
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\textsf{WellFormed}_{\Sigma}}
are still linear, but
not all words can be concatenated together: for instance,
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)

cannot be followed by
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Formally, given two words
u,v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\textsf{WellFormed}_{\Sigma}}
, to decide if
uv\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\textsf{WellFormed}_{\Sigma}}

it is necessary and sufficient to know if the last pair of
u
and first pair of
v

consists of a pair of proper letters (denoted by
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
), a pair of a proper letter and a blank/padding symbol (
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}
) or a pair of a blank/padding symbol and a proper letter (
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
). This information is called the
letter-type
of an element of
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!
.

We then define the
type
of a word of
({\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!)^{+}
as the pair
(\alpha,\beta)
,
usually written
\alpha\to\beta
, of the
letter-types
of its first and last letters.
It is then routine to check that the possible types of
well-formed words
are

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\mathcal{T}}\!\mathrel{\hat{=}}\big\{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt,\;{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt,\;{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt,\;{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt,\;{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big\}.

For the sake of readability, we will write
\alpha
instead of
\alpha\to\alpha

for
\alpha\in\{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt,{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt,{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\}
.

One non-trivial point lies in the following innocuous question: what is the
type
of the empty word? Any
type
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathcal{T}}\!
sounds like an acceptable answer. But then
it would be natural to say that the concatenation of
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}aaa\\
aaa\end{smallmatrix}\right)
of
type

\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
with the empty word of
type

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt
should be
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}aaa\\
aaa\end{smallmatrix}\right)
of
type

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt
. Automata-wise,
this would represent a sequence of transitions
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right)
together with
the promise that the next transition would have a padding symbol on its second tape. But then,
one has to formalize the idea that the two elements
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}aaa\\
aaa\end{smallmatrix}\right)
of type
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt

represent the same underlying pair of words of
\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}
: this idea will be captured by what we call a
dependency relation
. A more natural solution would be to simply introduce a
new type for the empty word (or to forbid it), but we show in
Appendix

F
that
the resulting notion of algebras cannot capture the property of being a
\mathcal{V}
-relation
.

A
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathcal{T}}\!
-typed set
(or
typed set
for short) consists of
a tuple
\mathbf{X}=(X_{\tau})_{\tau\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathcal{T}}\!}
, where each
X_{\tau}
is a set.
Instead of
x\in X_{\tau}
, we will often write
x_{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\tau}}\in\mathbf{X}
.
A
map between typed sets

\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Y}
is a collection of functions

X_{\tau}\to Y_{\tau}
for each
type

\tau
.
Similarly, a subset of
\mathbf{X}
is a tuple of subsets of
X_{\tau}
for each
type

\tau
.
To make the notations less heavy, we will often think of

typed sets
as sets with type annotations rather than tuples, and ask that
all operators/constructions should preserve this
type
.

Definition 8
.

A
dependency relation
over a
typed set

\mathbf{X}
consists of
a reflexive and symmetric relation
\mathrel{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\asymp}}
over

\mathop{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\text{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\LARGE$\uplus$}}}}\mathbf{X}\mathrel{\hat{=}}\bigcup_{\tau\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathcal{T}}\!}X_{\tau}\times\{\tau\}
,
such that for all
x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}},y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\in\mathbf{X}
,
if
x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}
,
then
x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}=y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}
.

Crucially, we do not ask for
this relation
to be transitive—in some examples the
dependency relation
will be an equivalence relation, but not always (
Example

32
), and
this non-transitivity is actually an important feature, motivated amongst other by the

syntactic congruence
and
Corollary

21
.

A
dependent set
is a
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathcal{T}}\!
-typed set
together with a

dependency relation
over it. A
closed subset
of a
dependent set

\langle\mathbf{X},\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}\rangle
is a subset
C\subseteq\mathbf{X}
such that for all

x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbf{X}
, if
x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}x^{\prime}
then
x\in C\iff x^{\prime}\in C
.
8
8

8

In other
words,
C
is a union of equivalence classes of the transitive closure of
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}
.

Figure 5:

Representation of the
dependent set

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
of
synchronous words
.
Coloured edges represent the
dependency relation
, and self-loops are not drawn.

Example 9
.

Given a finite alphabet
\Sigma
, let
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
be
9
9

9

The index refers to the arity of the relations we are considering: here we focus on binary relations, but all constructions can be generalized to higher arities.
the
dependent set
of
synchronous words
defined by:

•

({\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma})_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}\mathrel{\hat{=}}(\Sigma\times\Sigma)^{*}
,

•

({\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma})_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}\mathrel{\hat{=}}(\Sigma\times\Sigma)^{*}(\Sigma\times\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt)^{*}
,

•

({\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma})_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}\mathrel{\hat{=}}(\Sigma\times\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt)^{*}
,

•

({\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma})_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}\mathrel{\hat{=}}(\Sigma\times\Sigma)^{*}(\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\times\Sigma)^{*}
,

•

({\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma})_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}\mathrel{\hat{=}}(\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\times\Sigma)^{*}
.

Moreover,
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}
is the reflexive and symmetric closure of the relation that
identifies
u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
with
u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to\beta}}
for all

u\in(\Sigma\times\Sigma^{*})
and
\beta\in\{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt,{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\}
,
and
u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
with
u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}

for
u\in(\Sigma\times\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt)^{*}
,
and
u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
with
u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}

for
u\in(\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\times\Sigma)^{*}
.
This structure is depicted in
Figure

5
.

Given a
relation

\mathcal{R}\subseteq\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}
, we denote by

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}=\{(u,v)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\mid(u,v)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}\text{ and }(u,v)\in\mathcal{R}\}

the
closed subset
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
induced by
\mathcal{R}
.

Fact 10
.

The map
\mathcal{R}\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}
is a bijection between

relations
and
closed subsets
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
.

Proof.

Let
f
be the function which maps a closed subset
C
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
to

\{(u,v)\in\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}\mid(u,v)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\in C\text{ for some $\tau\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathcal{T}}\!$}\}
. It then follows that
f\circ{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{-}}
(resp.
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{f(-)}}
) is the identity
on subsets of
\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}
(resp.
closed subsets
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
).
∎

3.2
Synchronous Algebras

One key property of
types
is that some of them can be concatenated to produce other types.
We say that two types
\sigma,\tau\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathcal{T}}\!
are
compatible

when there exists non-empty words
u,v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\textsf{WellFormed}_{\Sigma}}
of
type

\sigma

and
\tau
, respectively, such that
uv
is
well-formed
.
Said otherwise,
\alpha\to\beta
is
compatible
with
\beta^{\prime}\to\gamma

if either
\beta=\beta^{\prime}
or
\beta={\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt
—indeed, for this last case
note that
e.g.
the concatenation of
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}aaa\\
aaa\end{smallmatrix}\right)
of type
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
with

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\\
aa\end{smallmatrix}\right)
of type
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
is
well-formed
.
Lastly, if
\alpha\to\beta
is
compatible with

\beta^{\prime}\to\gamma
,
we define their product as
(\alpha\to\beta)\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\cdot}(\beta^{\prime}\to\gamma)\mathrel{\hat{=}}\alpha\to\gamma
.
Note that this partial operation is associative, in the following sense: for
\rho,\sigma,\tau\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathcal{T}}\!
,
(\rho{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\cdot}\sigma){\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\cdot}\tau
is well-defined if and only if

\rho{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\cdot}(\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\cdot}\tau)
is well-defined, in which case both
types
are equal.
This implies that the notion of
compatibility
of types can be unambiguously
lifted to finite lists of
types

\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n}
.

Definition 11
.

A
synchronous algebra

\langle\mathbf{A},\cdot,\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}\rangle
consists of a
dependent set

\langle\mathbf{A},\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}\rangle
together
with a partial binary operation
\cdot
on
\mathbf{A}
, called
product
such that:

•

for
x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}},y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\in\mathbf{A}
,

x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\cdot y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}
is defined iff
\sigma
and
\tau

are
compatible
,

•

associativity:
for all
x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\rho}},y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}},z_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\in\mathbf{A}
, if
\rho,\sigma,\tau
are
compatible
:

(x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\rho}}\cdot y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}})\cdot z_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}=x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\rho}}\cdot(y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\cdot z_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}),

•

“monotonicity”:
for all
x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}},x^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma^{\prime}}},y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\in\mathbf{A}
, if
x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}x^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma^{\prime}}}
and
both
\sigma,\tau
and
\sigma^{\prime},\tau
are
compatible
, then

x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\cdot y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}x^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma^{\prime}}}\cdot y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}
,
and dually if
\tau,\sigma
and
\tau,\sigma^{\prime}
are
compatible
, then

y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\cdot x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\cdot x^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma^{\prime}}}
,

•

units:
for each type
\tau
there is an element
1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\in\mathbf{A}

such that for any
x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\in\mathbf{A}
, then

1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\cdot x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}

if
\tau
and
\sigma
are
compatible
,
and
x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\cdot 1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}

if
\sigma
and
\tau
are
compatible
,
and moreover,
1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to\beta}}=1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\cdot 1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\beta}}

for
\beta\in\{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt,{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\}
.

Note in particular that for any type
\tau\in\{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt,{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt,{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\}
,
then
1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\cdot x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}

but since
1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\cdot x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}
has
type

\tau
and
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}
is a

dependency relation
, then
1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\cdot x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}=x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}
.
This implies in particular that restricting
\langle\mathbf{A},\cdot\rangle

to a type
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
,
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}
or
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
yields a monoid.
These are called the three
underlying monoids
of
\mathbf{A}
.
The canonical example of
synchronous algebras
is
synchronous words

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
under
concatenation. Its
underlying monoids
are
(\Sigma\times\Sigma)^{*}
,

(\Sigma\times\{\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\})^{*}
and
(\{\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\times\Sigma)^{*}
.

Fact 12
.

Any
closed subset
of
\mathbf{A}
either contains all units, or none of them.

Proof.

From
1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}=1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\cdot 1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
we have

1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}

and
1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
.
By symmetry between
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}
and
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
, we also have

1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
and
1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
.
Hence, if a
closed subset
of
\mathbf{A}
contains at least one unit, then it must contain them all.
∎

Note that the
product
induces a monoid left (resp. right) action of the

underlying monoid

\mathbf{A}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}
(resp.
\mathbf{A}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}
) on the set
\mathbf{A}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}
.
Moreover,
x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\mapsto x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\cdot 1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}

identifies any element of
type

\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
with an element of
type

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt
.
Over
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
, these identifications are injective, but it need not be the
case in general. Note also that in general,

x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\cdot 1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}=x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\cdot 1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\cdot 1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}=x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\cdot 1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
.

Remark 13
.

There exists a monad over the category of
dependent sets
whose

Eilenberg-Moore algebras
exactly correspond to

synchronous algebras
, see
Appendix

A
.

Morphisms of synchronous algebras
are defined naturally as
maps that preserve the
type
, units, the
product
and the
dependency relation
.

Free algebras

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
is free in the sense that
for any
synchronous algebra

\mathbf{A}
, there is a natural bijection between

synchronous algebra morphisms

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}\to\mathbf{A}

and
maps of typed sets

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!\to\mathbf{A}
.
Said otherwise,
synchronous algebra morphisms
are uniquely defined by
their value on
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!
.

3.3
Recognizability

Given a
synchronous algebra

\mathbf{A}
, a
morphism

\varphi\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}\to\mathbf{A}

and a
closed subset

\textrm{Acc}\subseteq\mathbf{A}
called “accepting set”,
we say that
\langle\varphi,\mathbf{A},\textrm{Acc}\rangle

recognizes
a relation
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}

when
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}=\varphi^{-1}[\textrm{Acc}]
.
We extend the notion of
recognizability
to
\langle\varphi,\mathbf{A}\rangle

or to simply
\mathbf{A}
by existential quantification over the missing elements in the tuple
\langle\varphi,\mathbf{A},\textrm{Acc}\rangle
.

Synchronous algebra induced by a monoid

A monoid morphism
\varphi\colon({\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!)^{*}\to M

naturally
induces
a
synchronous algebra morphism

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\smash{\tilde{\varphi}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}\to\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\mathbf{A}_{M}}
,
where:

•

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{A}_{M}}
has for every
type

\tau
a copy of
M
,
and
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}
is
\{(x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}},x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}})\mid x\in M,\sigma,\tau\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathcal{T}}\!\}
,

•

for all
x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}},y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{A}_{M}}

with
compatible type
,
x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\cdot y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\mathrel{\hat{=}}(x\cdot y)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\cdot}\tau}}
,

•

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\smash{\tilde{\varphi}}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mathrel{\hat{=}}\bigl(\varphi\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right)\bigr)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\smash{\tilde{\varphi}}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mathrel{\hat{=}}\bigl(\varphi\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)\bigr)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\smash{\tilde{\varphi}}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mathrel{\hat{=}}\bigl(\varphi\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right)\bigr)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
.

The algebra simply duplicates
M
as many times as needed and identifies
two elements together when they originated from the same element of
M
.

Fact 14
.

If
\varphi
recognizes
\mathcal{R}
for some
relation

\mathcal{R}\subseteq\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}
seen as a language over
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.51994pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.42224pt\rule{2.53339pt}{0.6333pt}\hskip 0.42224pt
}}}}\!
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\smash{\tilde{\varphi}}}

recognizes

\mathcal{R}
.

Consolidation of a synchronous algebra

Given a
synchronous algebra morphism

\varphi\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}\to\mathbf{A}
,
define its
consolidation
10
10

10

Named by analogy with Tilson’s construction
[
Til87
, 3, p. 102]
.
as the semigroup morphism

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\varphi^{\smash{0}}}\colon({\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!)^{*}\to{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\mathbf{A}^{\smash{0}}}
, where

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{A}^{\smash{0}}}
is the monoid obtained from

\mathop{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\LARGE$\uplus$}}}}{\mathbf{A}}
by first merging units,
by adding a zero (denoted by
0
), and extending
\cdot

to be a total function by letting all missing products equal
0
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\varphi^{\smash{0}}}
sends a word
u\in({\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.43494pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.39864pt\rule{2.3917pt}{0.59789pt}\hskip 0.39864pt
}}}}\!)^{*}
to

•

0
if
u
is not
well-formed
,

•

\varphi(u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}})
if
u\in(\Sigma\times\Sigma)^{*}
,

•

\varphi(u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}})
if
u\in(\Sigma\times\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt)^{+}
,

•

\varphi(u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}})
if
u\in(\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\times\Sigma)^{+}
,

•

\varphi(u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}})
if
u\in(\Sigma\times\Sigma)^{+}(\Sigma\times\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt)^{+}
,

•

\varphi(u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}})
if
u\in(\Sigma\times\Sigma)^{+}(\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\times\Sigma)^{+}
.

Note that this operation disregards the
dependency relation
of
\mathbf{A}
.

Fact 15
.

If
\varphi

recognizes
some
relation

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\varphi^{\smash{0}}}
recognizes
\mathcal{R}
, when seen as
a language over
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.51994pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.42224pt\rule{2.53339pt}{0.6333pt}\hskip 0.42224pt
}}}}\!
.

The following result follows from
14
,
15
and
4
.

Proposition 16
.

A
relation
is
synchronous
if and only if it is
recognized

by a finite
synchronous algebra
.

Let us continue with a slightly less trivial example of
algebra
.

Example 17

(Group relations:
Example

6
, cont’d.)
.

Fix
p,q\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
. Let
\mathbf{Z}_{p,q}
denote the algebra whose
underlying monoids
are:

•

the trivial monoid
(0,+)
for
type

\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
,

•

the cyclic monoid
(\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z},+)
for
type

\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}
,

•

the cyclic monoid
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z},+)
for
type

\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
.

Moreover, the sets
Z_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}
and
Z_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}
are defined
as
\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z}
, respectively.
The product is addition—we identify
0_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
with the zero of
\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}
and
of
\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z}
. We denote by
\bar{k}
the equivalence class of
k\in\mathbb{Z}
in

\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
when
n
is clear from context.
The
dependency relation
identifies (1) all units together and (2)
x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}
with
1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\cdot x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}
and
x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\cdot 1_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}
when the
types
are
compatible
.

Let
\varphi\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}\to\mathbf{Z}_{p,q}
be the
synchronous algebra morphism

defined by

\varphi\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mathrel{\hat{=}}\bar{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}},\quad\varphi\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mathrel{\hat{=}}\bar{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}},\quad\varphi\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mathrel{\hat{=}}\bar{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi(\varepsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}})\mathrel{\hat{=}}\bar{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\text{ for $\tau\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathcal{T}}\!$}.

This
morphism
recognizes any
relation
of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{I,J}\mathrel{\hat{=}}\big\{(u,v)\;\big|\;

\displaystyle|u|>|v|\text{ and }(|u|-|v|\bmod{p})\in I,\text{ or}

\displaystyle|u|<|v|\text{ and }(|v|-|u|\bmod{q})\in J.\hphantom{\text{ or}}\big\},

where
I\subseteq\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}
and
J\subseteq\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z}
are such that
\bar{0}\not\in I

and
\bar{0}\not\in J
. This last condition is necessary because the accepting set
has to be a
closed subset
of
\mathbf{Z}_{p,q}
: if
\bar{0}
was in
I
, then we would need

\bar{0}\in J
, but also to add
\bar{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
to the accepting set: this would
recognize

\displaystyle\big\{(u,v)\;\big|\;

\displaystyle|u|>|v|\text{ and }(|u|-|v|\bmod{p})\in I,\text{ or}

\displaystyle|u|<|v|\text{ and }(|v|-|u|\bmod{q})\in J,\text{ or }|u|=|v|\big\}.

Note also that all
relations

\mathcal{R}^{I,J}
with
\bar{0}\not\in I

and
\bar{0}\not\in J
are
group relations
: letting
G
be the group
\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z}
,

\mathcal{R}
can be written as
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\textsf{WellFormed}_{\Sigma}}\cap\psi^{-1}[I\times\{0\}\cup\{0\}\times J]
where
\psi\colon({\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.51994pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.42224pt\rule{2.53339pt}{0.6333pt}\hskip 0.42224pt
}}}}\!)^{*}\to G
is the monoid morphism defined by
\psi\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mathrel{\hat{=}}(\bar{0},\bar{0})
,
\psi\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mathrel{\hat{=}}(\bar{1},\bar{0})
and
\psi\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mathrel{\hat{=}}(\bar{0},\bar{1})
.

3.4
Syntactic Morphisms & Algebras

Lemma 18

(
Syntactic morphism theorem
)
.

For each relation
\mathcal{R}
, there exists a surjective
synchronous algebra morphism

{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\eta_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}\!}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\mathbf{A}_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}}

that
recognizes

\mathcal{R}
and is such that for any other
surjective
synchronous algebra morphism

\varphi\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{B}

recognizing

\mathcal{R}
, there exists a

synchronous algebra morphism

\psi\colon\mathbf{B}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{A}_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}}

such that the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{A}_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{B},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\eta_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}\!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

commutes.
The objects
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\eta_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}\!}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{A}_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}}
are called the
syntactic synchronous algebra morphism

and
syntactic synchronous algebra
of
\mathcal{R}
, respectively.
Moreover, these objects are unique up to
isomorphisms
of the
algebra
.

Corollary 19

(of
Propositions

16
and
18
)
.

A
relation
is
synchronous
if and only if its
syntactic synchronous algebra

is finite.

The proof of
Lemma

18
—see
Section

D.1
—relies,
as in the case of monoids, on the notion of congruence.

Given a
synchronous algebra

\langle\mathbf{A},\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}},\cdot\rangle
,
a
congruence
is any reflexive, symmetric relation
\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}
over
\mathbf{A}
which is
coarser than
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}
, and which is
locally transitive
,
meaning that for all
x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}},x^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}},y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}},y^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\in\mathbf{X}
,
if
x^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}
,

x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}
and

y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}y^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}
,
then
x^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}y^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}
.
11
11

11

In particular, it implies that

\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}
is transitive when restricted to elements of the same type.

The
quotient structure

\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}{\mathbf{A}}/{\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}}}

of
\mathbf{A}
by a
congruence

\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}
is defined as follows:

•

its underlying
typed set
consists of the
equivalence classes of
\mathbf{A}
under the equivalence relation

\{(x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}},y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}})\mid x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\}
,
such a class being abusively denoted by
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}[}x{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}]^{\smash{\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.79727pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{2.50348pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.0762pt}{\rule{4.78339pt}{0.63779pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}}$}}}}}}
,

•

its
product
is the
product
induced by
\mathbf{A}
, in the sense
that
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}[}x{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}]^{\smash{\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.79727pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{2.50348pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.0762pt}{\rule{4.78339pt}{0.63779pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}}$}}}}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}[}y{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}]^{\smash{\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.79727pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{2.50348pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.0762pt}{\rule{4.78339pt}{0.63779pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}}$}}}}}}\mathrel{\hat{=}}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}[}xy{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}]^{\smash{\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.79727pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{2.50348pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.0762pt}{\rule{4.78339pt}{0.63779pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}}$}}}}}}
, and

•

its
dependency relation
is the relation induced by
\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}
,

i.e.

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}[}x{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}]^{\smash{\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.79727pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{2.50348pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.0762pt}{\rule{4.78339pt}{0.63779pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}}$}}}}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}[}y{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}]^{\smash{\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.79727pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{2.50348pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.0762pt}{\rule{4.78339pt}{0.63779pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}}$}}}}}}
whenever
x\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}y
,

•

its units are defined as the equivalence classes of the units of
\mathbf{A}
.

Moreover,
x\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}[}x{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}]^{\smash{\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.79727pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{2.50348pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.0762pt}{\rule{4.78339pt}{0.63779pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}}$}}}}}}
defines a surjective

morphism of synchronous algebras
from
\mathbf{A}
to
