{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\mathbf{A}}/{\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}}}
.

Given a
synchronous algebra

\langle\mathbf{A},\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}},\cdot\rangle

and a
closed subset

C\subseteq\mathbf{A}
,
we define a
congruence

\mathrel{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{C}}}
, called

syntactic congruence
of
C
over
\mathbf{A}
by letting

a_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{C}}}b_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}
when for all
x,y\in\mathbf{A}

•

if both

xa_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}y

and
xb_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}y
are defined,
then
xa_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}y\in C

iff

xb_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}y\in C
, and

•

if both

xa_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}

and
xb_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}
are defined,
then
xa_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\in C

iff

xb_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\in C
, and

•

if both
a_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}y

and
b_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}y
are defined,
then
a_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}y\in C

iff

b_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}y\in C
.

It is routine to check that the
syntactic congruence
is indeed a
congruence
.
For instance, to prove that
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{C}}}
is coarser than
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}
, observe that
if
a_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}b_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}
, then
for all
x,y

s.t.
both
xa_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}y

and
xb_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}y
are defined, then
xa_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}y\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}xb_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}y
,
and since
C
is a
closed subset
of
\mathbf{A}
,
xa_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}y\in C

iff

xb_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}y\in C
. The other two conditions are proven in the same fashion.
Note however that while the relation is
locally transitive
, it is not
transitive in general.

When
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}
is a
relation
, we abuse
the notation and write
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}}
to denote
the
syntactic congruence

\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}}}}
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}

in
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
.
The existence of the
syntactic morphism
then follows from
the next proposition, proven in
Section

D.1
.

Proposition 20
.

Let
\varphi\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{A}
be a surjective
synchronous algebra morphism

that
recognizes

\mathcal{R}
, say
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}=\varphi^{-1}[\textrm{Acc}]

for some
closed subset

\textrm{Acc}\subseteq\mathbf{A}
, then

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\varphi}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.02228pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.37993pt}{\rule{6.13336pt}{0.81778pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}\colon

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}

\twoheadrightarrow

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\mathbf{A}}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.02228pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.37993pt}{\rule{6.13336pt}{0.81778pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}

u

\mapsto

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}[}\varphi(u){\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}]^{\smash{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8445pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{2.50348pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.13995pt}{\rule{5.06677pt}{0.67557pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}}

is the
syntactic morphism
of
\mathcal{R}
.

Corollary 21
.

In the
syntactic synchronous algebra

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{A}_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}}
, the
syntactic
congruence

\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.02228pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.37993pt}{\rule{6.13336pt}{0.81778pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}
and the
dependency relation

\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}

coincide.

Proof.

By
Proposition

20

applied to the
syntactic morphism
,

x\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}[}x{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}]^{\smash{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.79727pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{2.50348pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.0762pt}{\rule{4.78339pt}{0.63779pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}}

is an isomorphism from
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{A}_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{A}_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}}}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}
.
Hence,
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}[}x{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}]^{\smash{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.79727pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{2.50348pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.0762pt}{\rule{4.78339pt}{0.63779pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}[}y{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}]^{\smash{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.79727pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{2.50348pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.0762pt}{\rule{4.78339pt}{0.63779pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}}

in
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{A}_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}}}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}

iff

x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}y
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{A}_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}}
, for all
x,y\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{A}_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}}
.
But then, the
dependency relation

\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}
of

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{A}_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}}}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}
is, by definition,
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}[}x{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}]^{\smash{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.79727pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{2.50348pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.0762pt}{\rule{4.78339pt}{0.63779pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}[}y{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}]^{\smash{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.79727pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{2.50348pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.0762pt}{\rule{4.78339pt}{0.63779pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}}

iff

x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}y
.
Putting both equivalences together, we get that

x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}y

iff

x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}y
for all
x,y\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{A}_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}}
.
∎

We provide in
Example

32

a simple example of
syntactic synchronous algebra
whose
dependency relation

is not an equivalence relation.

Boolean operations

Given two
synchronous algebras

\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
, define their
Cartesian product

\mathbf{A}\times\mathbf{B}
by taking, for each type
\tau
, the Cartesian product
A_{\tau}\times B_{\tau}
.
Units,
product
are defined naturally, and the
dependency relation
is defined
by taking the conjunction over each component.
Then
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\neg}\mathcal{R}
is
recognized
by
\mathbf{A}
, and

\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{S}
are
recognized
by
\mathbf{A}\times\mathbf{B}
.

4
The Lifting Theorem & Pseudovarieties

4.1
Elementary Formulation

Example 22

(Group relations:
Example

17
cont’d)
.

We want to decide when the relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{I,J}\mathrel{\hat{=}}\big\{(u,v)\;\big|\;

\displaystyle|u|>|v|\text{ and }(|u|-|v|\bmod{p})\in I,\text{ or}

\displaystyle|u|<|v|\text{ and }(|v|-|u|\bmod{q})\in J.\hphantom{\text{ or}}\big\}

from
Example

17
is a
group relation
.
By definition this happens if and only if there exists a finite group
G
,
together with a monoid morphism
\varphi\colon({\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.24582pt\raisebox{1.51994pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.42224pt\rule{2.53339pt}{0.6333pt}\hskip 0.42224pt
}}}}\!)^{*}\to G
and a subset

\textrm{Acc}\subseteq G
s.t.
\forall u\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\textsf{WellFormed}_{\Sigma}}
,
u\in\mathcal{R}^{I,J}
iff
\varphi(u)\in\textrm{Acc}
.
We claim:

\mathcal{R}^{I,J}\text{ is a {\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}group relation}}\quad\text{{iff}}\quad\big(\bar{0}\not\in I\text{ and }\bar{0}\not\in J\big).

()

The right-to-left implication was shown in
Example

17
.
We prove the implication from left to right:
let
n
be the order of
G
so that
x^{n}=1
for all
x\in G
. In particular, we have:

\varphi\bigl(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{pqn}\bigr)=1=\varphi\bigl(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{pqn}\bigr)
.
Since
\varphi\bigl(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{pqn}\bigr)\not\in\mathcal{R}^{I,J}
, it follows that

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{pqn}\not\in\mathcal{R}^{I,J}

i.e.
\bar{0}\not\in I
. Also,
\bar{0}\not\in J
by symmetry, which concludes the proof.

Even more generally, we can decide if a
relation

\mathcal{R}
is a
group relation

by simply looking at the
syntactic synchronous algebra
of
\mathcal{R}
.

\liftingtheoremmonoids

See the proof in
Section

E.1
.

Remark 23
.

In light of
Section

1.3
,
one can wonder whether the notion of
synchronous algebra
is necessary to
characterize
\mathcal{V}
-relations
, or if it is enough to look at the languages corresponding to
the
underlying monoids
.
Said otherwise, is the membership of
\mathcal{R}
in the class of
\mathcal{V}
-relations
uniquely
determined by the regular languages
\mathcal{R}\cap(\Sigma\times\Sigma)^{*}
,

\mathcal{R}\cap(\Sigma\times\{\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\})^{*}
and
\mathcal{R}\cap(\{\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\times\Sigma)^{*}
?
Unsurprisingly,
synchronous algebras
are indeed necessary, as
there are relations
\mathcal{R}
such that:

\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}\cap(\Sigma\times\Sigma)^{*}\in\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma\times\Sigma},\quad{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}\cap(\Sigma\times\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt)^{*}\in\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma\times\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt}\quad\text{and}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}\cap(\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\times\Sigma)^{*}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\times\Sigma},

()

but
\mathcal{R}
is
not
a
\mathcal{V}
-relation
. This can happen even if

\mathcal{V}
is the
\ast
-pseudovariety
of all regular languages: for instance for the

relation

\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\hat{=}}\{(u,v)\mid|u|>|v|>0\text{ and }|u|-|v|\text{ is prime}\}.

Notice that there is a subtle but crucially important difference between
(
()
‣
23
) and the second item of the
Lifting Theorem
:
while the
underlying monoids
of a
synchronous algebra

\mathbf{A}

recognizing

\mathcal{R}

only accept words of the form
(\Sigma\times\Sigma)^{*}
,
(\Sigma\times\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt)^{*}

or
(\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\times\Sigma)^{*}
, elements of
(\Sigma\times\Sigma)^{+}(\Sigma\times\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt)^{+}

or
(\Sigma\times\Sigma)^{+}(\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\times\Sigma)^{+}
influence the
underlying monoids
of
\mathbf{A}

via the axioms of
synchronous algebras
.

Also, note that the existence the
Lifting Theorem
follows from the careful
definition of
synchronous algebras
: more naive definitions of these algebras
simply cannot characterize
\mathcal{V}
-relations
, see
Appendix

F
.

From
Section

1.3

and the implicit fact that all our constructions are effective,
we obtain a decidability (meta-)result for

\mathcal{V}
-relations
.

Corollary 24
.

The class of
\mathcal{V}
-relations
has decidable membership if, and only if,
\mathcal{V}
has decidable membership.

For instance, a
relation
is a
group relation
if, and only if, all

underlying monoids
of its
syntactic synchronous algebra
are
groups.

4.2
Pseudovarieties of Synchronous Relations

We introduce the notion of
pseudovariety of synchronous algebras

and
\ast
-pseudovariety of synchronous relations
. We show an
Eilenberg correspondence
between these two notions. We then reformulate the
Lifting Theorem

to show that any
Eilenberg correspondence
between monoids and regular languages
lifts to an
Eilenberg correspondence
between
synchronous algebras
and
synchronous relations
.

Say that a
synchronous algebra

\mathbf{A}
is a
quotient
of
\mathbf{B}

when there exists a surjective
synchronous algebra morphism
from
\mathbf{B}
to
\mathbf{A}
.
A
subalgebra
of
\mathbf{B}
is any
closed subset
of
\mathbf{B}
closed under
product

and containing the units.
We then say that
synchronous algebra

\mathbf{A}

divides

\mathbf{B}

when
\mathbf{A}
is a
quotient
of a
subalgebra
of
\mathbf{B}
.

Observe that
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
admits the following property:
elements of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt
are generated by the
underlying monoids
.
Since
syntactic synchronous algebras
are homomorphic images of
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
, they also
satisfy this property. In general, we say that a
synchronous algebra

\mathbf{A}
is
locally
generated
if every element of type
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt
(resp.
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt
)
can be written as the product of an element of type
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
with an element of type
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}
(resp.
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
).

A
pseudovariety of synchronous algebras
is any class
\mathbb{V}

of
locally generated
finite
synchronous algebras
closed under

•

finite product:
if
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{V}
then
\mathbf{A}\times\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{V}
,

•

division:
if some finite
locally generated
algebra
\mathbf{A}

divides

\mathbf{B}
for some
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{V}
, then
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{V}
.

Because of
Lemma

18
, a
synchronous relation
is
recognized

by a finite synchronous algebra of a
pseudovariety

\mathbb{V}

iff

its
syntactic synchronous algebra
belongs to
\mathbb{V}
.

A
\ast
-pseudovariety of synchronous relations
is a function
\mathcal{V}\colon\Sigma\mapsto\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}

such that for any finite alphabet
\Sigma
,
\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}
is a set of
synchronous relations
over

\Sigma
such that
\mathcal{V}
is closed under

•

Boolean combinations
:
if
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S}\in\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}
, then

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\neg}\mathcal{R}
,
\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{S}
belong to
\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}
too,

•

Syntactic derivatives
:
if
\mathcal{R}\in\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}
, then any
relation

recognized
by the
syntactic synchronous algebra morphism
of
\mathcal{R}
also belongs
to
\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}
.

•

Inverse morphisms
:
if
\varphi\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Gamma}\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
is
a
synchronous algebra morphism
and
\mathcal{R}\in\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}
then

\varphi^{-1}[\mathcal{R}]\in\mathcal{V}_{\Gamma}
.

To recover a more traditional definition (of the form ‘‘closure under Boolean operations, residuals
12
12

12

Also called “quotient”
e.g.
in
[
Pin22
, III.1.3, p. 39]
, or “polynomial derivative” in
[
Boj15
, 4, p. 19]
.
and inverse morphisms”), we need to properly define what are the residuals of a
relation
. It
turns out that the answer is quite surprising and less trivial than what one would expect.

Definition 25

(Residuals)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a
synchronous algebra
,
x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\in\mathbf{A}
,
and
C\subseteq\mathbf{A}
be a
closed subset
.
The
left residual
and
right residual
of
C
by
x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}
are defined by

\displaystyle x^{{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}-1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}C

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hat{=}}\bigl\{y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\in\mathbf{A}\mid\exists y^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.02228pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.37993pt}{\rule{6.13336pt}{0.81778pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{C}}}y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}},\;x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}y^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau^{\prime}}}\in C\bigr\},\text{ and}

\displaystyle Cx^{{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}-1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hat{=}}\bigl\{y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\in\mathbf{A}\mid\exists y^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.02228pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.37993pt}{\rule{6.13336pt}{0.81778pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{C}}}y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}},\;y^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau^{\prime}}}x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\in C\bigr\},

respectively. We refer indiscriminately to both these notions as
residuals
.
We extend these notions to sets, by letting

X^{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}-1}}{}C\mathrel{\hat{=}}\bigcup_{x\in X}x^{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}-1}}{}C

and
CX^{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}-1}}{}\mathrel{\hat{=}}\bigcup_{x\in X}Cx^{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}-1}}{}
.

For the sake of readability, we will sometimes drop the
type
of elements when dealing
with
residuals
.
It is routine to check that
residuals
are always
closed subsets
(since
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{C}}}
is coarser than the
dependency relation
), or that
(x^{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}-1}}C)y^{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}-1}}=x^{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}-1}}(Cy^{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}-1}})
.
Equivalently,
Cx^{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}-1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}
can be defined as the smallest
closed subset

containing the “naive residual”

\bigl\{y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\in\mathbf{A}\mid y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}x_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\in C\bigr\}
.
This latter set is always contained in
Cx^{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}-1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}
(by reflexivity of
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{C}}}
),
and moreover, if it is empty, then so is
Cx^{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}-1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}
.

As an example, consider the relation
\mathcal{R}
from
Example

32
.
Then the “naive right residual” of
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}
by
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}

consists of
\varepsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
and all elements of type
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt
.
But it does not contain any element of type
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt
because such elements cannot be concatenated with
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
on the right.
Yet, the
residual

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}-1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
contains all elements of type
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
(and also
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt
): for instance,
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}-1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
since
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}

and
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\in\mathcal{R}
.

On the other hand, in the
algebra

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}a}
consider the relation

\mathcal{S}=(aa)^{*}\times a(aa)^{*}
.
Then
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{S}}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}-1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
is empty since its
“naive residual”
\{y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}a}\mid y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathcal{S}\}

is empty. Indeed, for
y_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
to
be well-defined, one needs
\tau
to be
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
,
i.e.

y
encodes a pair of
two words
(u,v)
of the same length. But then
(ua,va)\not\in\mathcal{S}
.

Lemma 26
.

A class
\mathcal{V}\colon\Sigma\mapsto\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}
is a
\ast
-pseudovariety of synchronous relations
if, and only if, it is closed under
Boolean combinations
,
residuals
and

inverse morphisms
.

See the proof in
Section

E.2
.

Let
\mathbb{V}\mathop{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\to}}\mathcal{V}
denote the map (called
correspondence
) that takes a

pseudovariety of synchronous algebras
and maps it to

\mathcal{V}\colon\Sigma\mapsto\{\mathcal{R}\subseteq\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{A}_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}}\in\mathbb{V}\}.

Dually, let
\mathcal{V}\mathop{\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\to}}\mathbb{V}
denote the
correspondence
that takes
a
\ast
-pseudovariety of synchronous relations

\mathcal{V}

and maps it to the
pseudovariety of synchronous algebras

generated
by
all
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{A}_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}}
for some
\mathcal{R}\in\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}
.
Here, the
pseudovariety generated
by a class
C

of finite
locally generated

synchronous algebras

is the smallest
pseudovariety
containing
all finite
locally generated

algebras
of
C
,
or equivalently,
13
13

13

The proof is straightforward,
see
e.g.

[
Pin22
, Proposition XI.1.1, p. 190]
for a proof in the context of semigroups.
the class of all finite
locally generated

synchronous algebras

that
divide
a finite product of
algebras
of
C
.
14
14

14

Note that “being
locally generated
” is not preserved by taking
subalgebras
, but this is not an issue: we restrict the construction to (finite)
locally generated

algebras
.

Lemma 27

(
An Eilenberg theorem for synchronous relations
)
.

The correspondences
\mathbb{V}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\to}}\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{V}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\to}}\mathbb{V}
define
mutually inverse bijections between
pseudovarieties of
synchronous algebras
and
\ast
-pseudovarieties of synchronous relations
.

See the proof in
Section

E.3
.

As consequence of
Lemma

27
, if

\mathcal{V}
is a
\ast
-pseudovariety of synchronous relations

and
\mathbb{V}
is a
pseudovariety of synchronous algebras
,
we write
\mathcal{V}\mathop{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\leftrightarrow}}\mathbb{V}

to mean that either
\mathcal{V}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\to}}\mathbb{V}
or, equivalently,
\mathbb{V}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\to}}\mathcal{V}
.
This relation is called an
Eilenberg-Schützenberger correspondence
.

Proposition 28
.

If
\mathbb{V}
is a
pseudovariety of monoids
, then

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\mathbb{V}^{\textrm{sync}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hat{=}}\{\mathbf{A}\text{ {\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}locally generated} finite {\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}synchronous algebra}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\text{ {s.t.} all {\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}underlying monoids} of $\mathbf{A}$ are in $\mathbb{V}$}\}

is a
pseudovariety of synchronous algebras
. Similarly,
if
\mathcal{V}
is an
\ast
-pseudovariety of regular languages
, then
the class of
\mathcal{V}
-relations
, namely

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\mathcal{V}^{\textrm{sync}}}\colon\Sigma\mapsto\{\mathcal{R}\subseteq\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}\mid\exists L\in\mathcal{V}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\Sigma^{\smash{2}}_{\smash{\hskip 0.22083pt\raisebox{0.91995pt}{\scriptsize\hskip 0.42224pt\rule{2.53339pt}{0.6333pt}\hskip 0.42224pt
}}}}\!},\,{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}=L\cap{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\textsf{WellFormed}_{\Sigma}}\},

is a
\ast
-pseudovariety of synchronous relations
.

Proof.

The first point is straightforward. The second one follows from it and
Lemmas

27
and
1.3
.
∎

Finally,
Section

1.3
can be elegantly rephrased
by saying that correspondences between
pseudovarieties of monoids

and
\ast
-pseudovarieties of regular languages
lift to correspondences
between
pseudovarieties of synchronous algebras
and

\ast
-pseudovarieties of synchronous relations
.

Theorem 29

(
Lifting Theorem: Pseudovariety Formulation
)
.

If, in the
Eilenberg correspondence

between
pseudovarieties of monoids
and
\ast
-pseudovarieties of regular languages

we have
\mathcal{V}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\leftrightarrow}}\mathbb{V}
,
then in the
Eilenberg correspondence

between the
pseudovariety of synchronous algebras

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbb{V}^{\textrm{sync}}}
and
the
\ast
-pseudovariety of synchronous relations

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathcal{V}^{\textrm{sync}}}
,
we have
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathcal{V}^{\textrm{sync}}}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\leftrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbb{V}^{\textrm{sync}}}
.

5
Discussion

A natural next step is to generalize
Question

2
by replacing

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\textsf{WellFormed}_{\Sigma}}
by a fixed regular language
\Omega
.

Question 30
.

Given a class of regular languages
\mathcal{V}
, can we characterize (and decide) all
languages of the form
L\cap\Omega
for some
L\in\mathcal{V}
?

We claim that the construction of
synchronous algebras

can be generalized for any
\Omega
, giving rise to the notion of “path algebras”.
The
lifting theorem for monoids
can be shown to hold for some
\Omega
, including
well-formed words
for
n
-ary relations with
n\geq 3
, and that it cannot effectively hold for all
\Omega
.

A natural next step would then be to study the relationship between “path algebras” and
Figueira & Libkin’s
L
-controlled relations
[
FL15
, 3]
,
see also
[
DFP18
]
.

Lastly, it would be interesting to extend the results on algebras to automata: for instance, can
we adapt our proof to show the existence of a minimal
synchronous
automaton for each relation?

Appendix A
Monads Everywhere!

We denote by
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\mathsf{Set}^{\smash{\mathcal{S}}}}
and
\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\mathsf{Pos}^{\smash{\mathcal{S}}}}
the categories of
\mathcal{S}
-typed sets
and
\mathcal{S}
-partially ordered sets—note that in this model,
each type is equipped with its own order and
that elements of different types cannot be compared.
Similarly, let
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\mathsf{Dep}^{\smash{\mathcal{S}}}}
be the category of
\mathcal{S}
-
dependent sets
.

We claim that
synchronous algebras
correspond to
Eilenberg-Moore algebras
of some monad over the category
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathsf{Dep}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathcal{T}}\!}}}
. For the sake of readability, we represent the underlying

typed set
of a

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathcal{T}}\!
-
dependent set

\mathbf{X}=\langle X_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt},\,X_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt},\,X_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt},\,X_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt},\,X_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}\rangle

as follows:
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}

\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}

\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}

X_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}
X_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}
X_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}
X_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}
X_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}.

We define the
synchronous monad

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.4141,0.1289,0.1211}\mathbb{S}_{2}}
over
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathsf{Dep}^{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathcal{T}}\!}}}
as the functor which maps

\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}

\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}

\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}

A
B
C
D
E

equipped with a dependency relation
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}
to the
dependent set
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}

\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}

\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}

A^{*}
A^{*}BC^{*}\cup A^{*}C^{*}
C^{*}
A^{*}DE^{*}\cup A^{*}E^{*}
E^{*},

and two words are
dependent
if their domain are isomorphic
and their letters are pairwise dependent.
The unit and free multiplication are defined naturally.

Note in particular that all five empty words are
mutually dependent
,
and that
synchronous words

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
correspond to applying

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbb{S}_{2}}
to
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}

\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}

\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}

\Sigma\times\Sigma
\varnothing
\Sigma\times\{\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\}
\varnothing
\{\hskip 0.50003pt\rule{3.00003pt}{0.74997pt}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\times\Sigma,

equipped with equality.
Moreover,
synchronous algebras
exactly correspond to
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbb{S}_{2}}
-algebras.

A systemic approach to algebraic language theory was proposed by Bojańczyk using the formalism of monads
[
Boj15
]
, for monads over finitely typed sets
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathsf{Set}^{\smash{\mathcal{S}}}}
.
Urbat, Adámek, Chen & Milius then extended these results to capture monads over varieties of typed (ordered) algebras
[
UACM17
]
.
Lastly, Blumensath extended those results to monads over the category of typed posets
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathsf{Pos}^{\smash{\mathcal{S}}}}

when the set
\mathcal{S}
of types is infinite
[
Blu21
]
.

Observe that the category of
dependent sets
is not captured by any of the results above since
the
dependency relation
can compare elements of different types, contrary to typed posets & co.

Appendix B
Characterizing Induced Classes of Recognizable Relations

In this section, we prove a simple characterization of

recognizable relations
whose languages are “simple”.

Proposition 31
.

Let
\mathbb{V}
be a pseudovariety of finite monoids
and
\mathcal{V}
be the corresponding pseudovariety of regular languages.
Given a relation
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}
, the following
are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{R}
is recognized by a monoid in
\mathbb{V}
, i.e.
there is a monoid morphism
\varphi\colon\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}\to M

for some monoid
M\in\mathbb{V}
such that
\mathcal{R}=\varphi^{-1}[\textrm{Acc}]
, and

2.

There exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
K_{1},\ldots,K_{n},L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}

in
\mathcal{V}
such that
\mathcal{R}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}K_{i}\times L_{i}
,

in which case we say that
\mathcal{R}
is
\mathcal{V}
-recognizable.

The statement of the result when
\mathbb{V}
is the pseudovariety of all regular languages is folklore,
see
e.g.

[
Sak09
, Corollary II.2.20, p. 254]
,
and the proof of the proposition is easy.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
.

By definition:

\mathcal{R}=\bigcup_{z\in\textrm{Acc}}\varphi^{-1}[z].

Observe then that
\varphi(u,v)=\varphi((u,\varepsilon)\cdot(\varepsilon,v))=\varphi((u,\varepsilon))\cdot\varphi((\varepsilon,v))
for all
u,v\in\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}
, and hence:

\mathcal{R}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in M\\
\text{{s.t.} }x\cdot y\in\textrm{Acc}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\{u\in\Sigma^{*}\mid\varphi(u,\varepsilon)=x\}}_{\mathrel{\hat{=}}\;K_{x}}\times\underbrace{\{v\in\Sigma^{*}\mid\varphi(\varepsilon,v)=y\}}_{\mathrel{\hat{=}}\;L_{y}}.

Since
M
is finite, the union is finite, and moreover, each
K_{x}
and
L_{y}
is
recognized by
M
, and hence belong to
\mathcal{V}
.

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
.
If
\mathcal{R}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}K_{i}\times L_{i}

where all languages belong to
\mathcal{V}
, then let
M_{i},N_{i}\in\mathbb{V}

be their syntactic monoid,
\zeta_{i},\eta_{i}
be their
syntactic morphism, and
\textrm{Acc}_{i},\textrm{Bcc}_{i}
be their accepting sets. Consider the monoid morphism

\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}

\to

\prod_{i}(M_{i}\times N_{i})

(u,v)

\mapsto

\bigl(\zeta_{i}(u),\eta_{i}(v)\bigr)_{i}
.

Then
\mathcal{R}
is the preimage by this morphism of

\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(\ldots\times(M_{i-1}\times N_{i-1})\times(\textrm{Acc}_{i}\times\textrm{Bcc}_{i})\times(M_{i+1}\times N_{i+1})\times\ldots\bigr).

The conclusion follows from the fact that
\mathbb{V}
is closed under (finite) products.
∎

Appendix D
Details on Synchronous Algebras

D.1
Proof of the Syntactic Morphism Theorem

See
18

Proof of
Lemma

18
.

Uniqueness.
Consider two potential
syntactic morphisms
,
say
\eta_{1}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{A}_{1}
and
\eta_{2}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{A}_{2}
.
Then by the universal property of
\eta_{1}
(resp.
\eta_{2}
), there exists

\psi_{1}\colon\mathbf{A}_{2}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{A}_{1}
and
\psi_{2}\colon\mathbf{A}_{1}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{A}_{2}
such that

\eta_{1}=\psi_{1}\circ\eta_{2}
and
\eta_{2}=\psi_{2}\circ\eta_{1}
. Overall, it implies that
the following digram commutes
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{A}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{A}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{A}_{1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{2}}

and so
\psi_{1}\circ\psi_{2}\circ\eta_{1}=\eta_{1}
. Since
\eta_{1}

is surjective, and hence right-cancellative,
\psi_{1}\circ\psi_{2}=\textrm{id}_{\mathbf{A}_{1}}
.
Symmetrically,
\psi_{2}\circ\psi_{1}=\textrm{id}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}
, showing that

\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
are mutually inverse
isomorphisms
of
synchronous algebras
.

Existence.
It follows from
Proposition

20
—which we will prove
just afterwards—applied to the identity morphism
\textrm{id}{}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
,
which
recognizes

\mathcal{R}
since
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}=\textrm{id}^{-1}[{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}]
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}

is
closed
.
∎

See
20

Proof.

We claim that
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\varphi}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}

is
a
, and hence
the
,

syntactic synchronous algebra morphism
of
\mathcal{R}
.
First, if
\varphi(u)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}\varphi(v)

then in particular
\varphi(u)\in\textrm{Acc}

iff

\varphi(v)\in\textrm{Acc}
.
It follows that the preimage of

\textrm{Acc}^{\prime}\mathrel{\hat{=}}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}[}\textrm{Acc}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}]^{\smash{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.79727pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{2.50348pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 3.98611pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.0762pt}{\rule{4.78339pt}{0.63779pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.98611pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}$}}\hskip-3.98611pt\hskip 3.98611pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}}
by
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\varphi}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}

is indeed
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}
. Moreover
\textrm{Acc}^{\prime}
is a
closed subset
of

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\mathbf{A}}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}
since
Acc
is a
closed subset
of
\mathbf{A}
.
Hence,
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\varphi}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}
is a surjective
morphism

which
recognizes

\mathcal{R}
.

Then, given another surjective
morphism

\psi\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{B}

which
recognizes

\mathcal{R}
, say
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}=\psi^{-1}[\textrm{Bcc}]
, then for
each
b_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\in\mathbf{B}
, there exists
u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
such that

\psi(u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}})=b_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}
. This defines a map

\chi\colon\mathbf{B}\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\mathbf{A}}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}
which
sends
b_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\varphi}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}(u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}})
.

We claim that
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\varphi}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}=\chi\circ\psi
,
meaning that the following diagram commutes
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{A}_{\smash{\mathcal{R}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{B}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{-}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.79727pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptstyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 2.39554pt\hskip-2.39554pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\frown$}\hskip-2.39554pt\hskip-1.67688pt\raisebox{1.2267pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\hbox{\hskip 1.67688pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\raisebox{0.32411pt}{\rule{1.44061pt}{0.19208pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-1.67688pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\smile$}}\hskip-1.67688pt\hskip 1.67688pt}$}}\hskip-1.67688pt\hskip 2.39554pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\varphi}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.79727pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptstyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 2.39554pt\hskip-2.39554pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\frown$}\hskip-2.39554pt\hskip-1.67688pt\raisebox{1.2267pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\hbox{\hskip 1.67688pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\raisebox{0.32411pt}{\rule{1.44061pt}{0.19208pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-1.67688pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\smile$}}\hskip-1.67688pt\hskip 1.67688pt}$}}\hskip-1.67688pt\hskip 2.39554pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}

Indeed, for

u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
,
\chi(\psi(u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}))
equals

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\varphi}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}(v_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}})
for some
v_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}

such that
\psi(v_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}})=\psi(u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}})
.
This in turns implies that
u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}}v_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}
since
for all
x,y\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
, if
xuy
and
xvy
are well-defined,
then so are
\psi(x)\psi(u)\psi(v)
and
\psi(x)\psi(v)\psi(y)
,
and both elements are equal, so one belongs to
Bcc

iff
the other does.
It follows that
xuy\in\mathcal{R}

iff

xvy\in\mathcal{R}
, and hence

u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}}v_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}
.
By surjectivity of
\varphi
, it follows that

\varphi(u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}\varphi(v_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}})
,

i.e.

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\varphi}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}(u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}})={\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\varphi}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}(v_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}})
.
And hence
\chi(\psi(u))={\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\varphi}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}(u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}})
.

We now show that
\chi
is a
morphism
.
From
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\varphi}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}=\chi\circ\psi
it follows that
the map
\chi
preserves the
product
15
15

15

See
e.g.

[
Boj15
, Lemma 3.2, p. 10]
.
and is surjective.
Lastly, we claim that it preserves the
dependency relation
. Indeed,
by surjectivity of
\psi
, it boils down to proving that
for all
u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}},v_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
, if
\psi(u)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}\psi(v)
then

\chi(\psi(u))\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}\chi(\psi(v))
, namely
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\varphi}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}(u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\varphi}/{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}}}(v_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}})
, which
rewrites as
\varphi(u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}\varphi(v_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}})
.
So pick
x,y
such that both
xuy
and
xvy
are well-defined. Then

\psi(x)\psi(u)\psi(y)
and
\psi(u)\psi(v)\psi(y)
have the same
type

as
xuy
and
xvy
, respectively, so they are well-defined,
but since
\psi(u)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}\psi(v)
, then
\psi(x)\psi(u)\psi(y)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\asymp}}\psi(x)\psi(u)\psi(y)

and since
Bcc
is a
closed subset
, one of these elements belongs in
it
iff
the other ones does too, from which it follows
that
xuv\in\mathcal{R}

iff

xvy\in\mathcal{R}

i.e.

u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}}v_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}}

and hence
\varphi(u_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\sigma}})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.34995pt}{\rule{6.00006pt}{0.80002pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\textrm{Acc}}}}\varphi(v_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\tau}})
.
Overall, this proves that
\chi
is a surjective
synchronous algebra morphism
,
and concludes our proof.
∎

D.2
A Syntactic Synchronous Algebra

Example 32
.

We now provide an example of
syntactic synchronous algebra
whose
dependency relation

is not an equivalence relation.
Consider the relation

\mathcal{R}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\Sigma\\
\Sigma\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{*}\bigl[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\Sigma\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\Sigma\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{+}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\\
\Sigma\end{smallmatrix}\right)+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\\
\Sigma\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{+}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right)\bigr],

where
\Sigma=\{a,b\}
.
In other words, a pair
(u,v)
belongs to
\mathcal{R}
if either:
the length difference between
u
and
v
is one, or
it is at least two and the longer words ends with an ‘
a
’.
We are going to compute the
syntactic congruence

\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.02228pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.37993pt}{\rule{6.13336pt}{0.81778pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}}
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\underline{\mathcal{R}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathbf{S}_{2}\Sigma}
.

Figure 6
:

The three
underlying monoids
of the
syntactic synchronous algebra
of the relation

\mathcal{R}
of
Example

32
, together with its
dependency relation
. The two equivalence classes of its transitive closure are drawn in red and blue.

Recall that the restriction of
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.02228pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.37993pt}{\rule{6.13336pt}{0.81778pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}}
to a single
type
is
an equivalence relation. For
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
, there is a single equivalence class,
denoted by/identified with
\varepsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
.
For
type

\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}
, we claim that for any
u\in\Sigma^{*}
:

•

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.02228pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.37993pt}{\rule{6.13336pt}{0.81778pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
iff
u\in\Sigma^{*}a
;

•

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.02228pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.37993pt}{\rule{6.13336pt}{0.81778pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
iff

;

•

\varepsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
is alone in its equivalence class;

•

all elements of the form
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
where
u
is a word of length at least 2 whose last letter is a ‘
b
’ are pairwise equivalent, and the class is identified with
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}bb\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
.

Since
\mathcal{R}
is invariant under
(u,v)\mapsto(v,u)
, the situation is symmetric for
type

\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{R}
is invariant under adding/removing prefixes of type
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
,
so
types

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt
also have four elements each.

In the end, we obtain the
synchronous algebra
drawn in
Figure

6
(elements of
type

{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt\to{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt
are omitted for the sake of simplicity).
Note that the
dependency relation
is not transitive:
for instance
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.02228pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.37993pt}{\rule{6.13336pt}{0.81778pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}

and
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.02228pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.37993pt}{\rule{6.13336pt}{0.81778pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}

but
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\not\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.02228pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.37993pt}{\rule{6.13336pt}{0.81778pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}
.

Given two elements of distinct
type
, we want to determine
when they are
dependent
. For the sake of simplicity,
we will focus on types
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt,{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 1.00006pt
and
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{b}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
.

Types
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}
and
\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}
.

Since
dependent
elements must either both belong to
\mathcal{R}
or both to
{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\neg}\mathcal{R}
,
we have
\varepsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\not\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.02228pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.37993pt}{\rule{6.13336pt}{0.81778pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
\hskip 0.40974pt\rule{2.45836pt}{0.61455pt}\hskip 0.40974pt\end{smallmatrix}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{b}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}

and
\varepsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\not\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.02228pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.37993pt}{\rule{6.13336pt}{0.81778pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\

Moreover,
\varepsilon_{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\text{$\nicefrac{{\footnotesize\textsc{l}\,}}{{\,\footnotesize\textsc{l}}}$}}\hskip 0.81949pt}}\not\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0859,0.2891,0.3477}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.02228pt}{\scalebox{.6}{$\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\frown$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{3.5764pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\raisebox{1.37993pt}{\rule{6.13336pt}{0.81778pt}}$}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{$\smile$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}$}}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt}}$}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}bb\\

\displaystyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
